**What remains for the final citation:** the equation *James Hawkes b. 1752 (the 1887 chart) = James Hawks m. Olive Willias 1776 (the record)* — closers: the 1790/1800 Petersham census (James Hawks's household), the 1887 chart's detail on James's marriage, and the WikiTree profile update (James & Olive attached as Edmund's parents — which the user can enter directly).
- **THE FAMILY'S OWN GENEALOGY DOCUMENT — typed copy of Nathan M. Hawks, "Gleanings Relative to the Family of Adam Hawks," *Essex Institute Historical Collections* 24 (1887), saved with the Bible [family-archive primary; the underlying article from the original Lynn records, A/S-grade]:**
  - **The Allerton descent as the family's own printed tradition (1887):** *"The descendants of this John Hawkes can trace their ancestry to the immortal compact signed in the cabin of the Mayflower. The wife of John was Rebecca, daughter of Moses Maverick, the founder and for many years the only magistrate of Marblehead. The wife of Moses Maverick was the daughter of Isaac Allerton, who was one of the Mayflower passengers and was Lieutenant Governor of Plymouth Colony…"*
  - **The martyr tradition, also in print (1887):** *"…their blood mingled with that of the successors of Thomas Hawkes, who was burned at the stake in the reign of 'Bloody Queen Mary,' for his faithfulness to his religious principles, and made a race fit to struggle for a new world."*
  - **The immigrant's probate, transcribed from the original records:** Adam Hawkes, d. 1671 — inventory taken 18 March 1671/72, totaling **£817 11s** (two muskets and two fowling pieces; three swords, a watch-bill, a pistol and a drum; "a bible and other books"; four oxen; seven cows; two horses; sixteen swine; the dwelling house and barn; bees; tobacco; **five hundred and thirty acres**); and the **Articles of Agreement dividing the estate, 27 March 1672** — administrator John Hawks; and the clause naming **"Moses Hawks, his son, which he had by Rebeckah Hawks, daughter of Mr. Moses Mavericke,"** with the line *"this aforesaid gift is the legacy of Mr. Adam Hawks to his grandchild Moses Hawks"* — witnesses **Sarah Hawks, Moses Mavericke, John Hawks**; approved in court.
  - **The relics recorded:** the first house's chimney brick **inscribed 1601, "evidently written in the soft clay with the finger when the brick was made in England,"** relaid in the fourth chimney by Richard Hawks of the sixth generation; the iron **fireback moulded with arms — "concluded to be some coat of arms, perhaps that of the Hawkes family" — "now guarded as an heirloom"** (1887 — survival to be asked after); the stone wall built *"by John, the son of the first settler, in 1688."*
  - **The chart's footnotes, transcribed in full (the family's own scholarship, warts and all):** *"* Daughter of Moses Maverick, founder of Marblehead." / "+ Children after Moses were by a second wife."* — and the Brewster passage, which preserves the era's most famous name-confusion in the family's own hand: *"Patience Brewster, daughter of Elder William Brewster, married Isaac Allerton — their daughter married Moses Maverick. (Memoir Plymouth Colony) Baylie's History of New Plymouth, Boston, 1866, says in foot note, page 70, Part 2, Vol. 1, that Patience says Patience married Gov. Prince in 1624, but on page 6 in foot note says Patience Brewster married Isaac Allerton, 'one of the most distinguished among the pilgrims.' Same book, page 131, foot note says: 'On the fifth of August, 1624, Mr. Thomas Prince, who was afterwards governor, was married to Mrs. Patience Brewster.'"* — the document thus carries BOTH the Allerton claim AND Baylie's contradictory footnotes (the Fear/Patience sister-confusion modern scholarship resolved: **Fear** Brewster married Isaac Allerton; **Patience** Brewster married Governor Thomas Prence). *The 1887 typist was scrupulous even where the sources conflicted — a model this file has tried to follow.*
- **The chart, 1st–6th generations:** Adam Hawkes (d. 1671) → **John m. (1) Rebecca Maverick\*, (2) Susannah** → **Moses, b. 1659, m. Margaret Cogswell, d. 1709** (the Allerton-line heir; by the second wife: Adam b. 1664, John, Thomas b. 1673, Mary b. 1675, Ebenezer) → 4th: Moses b. 1699, Margaret b. 1700, Adam b. 1702, **John b. 1705 m. Susannah Townsend**, Rebecca b. 1709 → 5th (children of John & Susannah): **Moses b. 1730**, Mary b. 1732, Susannah b. 1736, Abijah b. 1739, Anna b. 1742, Nathan b. 1745, Lois b. 1747, **Daniel b. 1749**, James b. 1752 → 6th (detailed only for **Nathan m. Sarah Hitchings**: Hannah b. 1773, Nathan b. 1775, Daniel b. 1779, Susannah b. 1782, Mary b. 1784, Moses b. 1788, Aaron b. 1791).
  - **⚠ What the chart does and does not settle:** it documents the family's own line to the Allerton marriage and gives the Society-patriot's birth year (**Daniel b. 1749 — the Mayflower Society's Daniel Hawkes was born at Lynn, 20 Oct 1749**), but it differs from the Society on Daniel's parents (chart: John & Susannah Townsend; Society: Moses & Susanna Hichins — flagged) and it does **not** detail Judge Edmund's branch (the chart follows contributor Nathan M. Hawks's own line; Moses's death is given as 1709 vs. 1741 elsewhere — flagged). **Judge Edmund (b. Oct. 9, 1780) remains one document from closure: the Petersham vital record.**
- **The Hawkes family Bible** (physical description): a thick (4–5 inch) family Bible; the names remembered in it are **Hawkes names** (further contents unconfirmed — see ledger). Physically, a Bible of that bulk is almost certainly a mid-19th-century subscription "illustrated family Bible" (c. 1840–1890, c. 1,200–1,800 pages plus plates, printed family-register leaves between the Testaments — the pages where family births/marriages/deaths were recorded). Likely publishers of that format: D. Fanshaw (NY), E.H. Butler & Co. (Philadelphia), A.S. Barnes (NY), William Collins (c. 1860), George Virtue, later H.J. Smith (Chicago). The user recalls that the Tower of Babel plate **folds out and is in color** (unconfirmed). This is now diagnostic to the plate level: the standard Tower of Babel engraving of the era is the **Kinnersley plate — "Tower of Babel. Published by T. Kinnersly, Jan. 30, 1818"** — documented as bound into (1) the Brightly & Kinnersley *Most Superb Folio and Self-Interpreting Bible* (New York, 1813; plates 1812–1818; issued in 162 monthly parts; tall thick folio), (2) Kinnersley's 1822 stereotyped edition (the first stereotyped Bible printed in the US), and (3) the **Isaac Collins-lineage quartos** — including a printing **sold at Auburn and Buffalo, NY (John E. Beardsley, 1820s–30s)**, squarely in the family's western-New-York corridor. The plate's own publication line (bottom margin) identifies it from one photograph. A hand-colored Babel *frontispiece* is documented in the thick quartos of the **Isaac Collins stereotype lineage** (Trenton 1791; reprinted by Holbrook at Brattleborough VT 1816/1817/1821, Andrus at Hartford, Carey at Philadelphia — the standard American family Bible of 1810–1840, often "with Canne's Marginal Notes" and Brown's Concordance), while steel-engraved Babel plates sit among the 20–40 plates of the mid-century illustrated family Bibles (Fanshaw/Butler/Virtue; the 1867 Virtue, Yorkston & Co. *Devotional Family Bible* is a documented example). A Collins-lineage quarto would fit the family's Massachusetts years (acquired before the Petersham→Oswego migration); a cloth-bound illustrated Bible fits an Oswego purchase of 1845–1875. Edition identification requires only two photographs: the **title page** and the **frontispiece/plate list**; dating tells: steel engravings → 1840s–60s, Doré-style illustrations → 1866+, color plates → 1870s+. Capture protocol: title page → every family-register page (straight-on, both sides; note changes of handwriting) → all flyleaf inscriptions ("Presented to…" entries often name the parent-to-child hand-off) → frontispiece and plate list → covers/spine under angled light. If the Bible's Moses Hawkes matches **Moses Hawks b. 1779, Petersham** (Daniel Hawkes 1749's documented son), the artifact itself closes the Allerton line.
- ***Mike's Story*** (James K. DeArmond): see §12.
- The **1850 census** (June; ten weeks after Judge Edmund's death) and the **1855 NY state census** (the household including Hannah Griggs Wilber) — both public, both unexamined as images.

## 13A. Deep-Dive Pass — 28 September 2026 (runs 12+): The Willis–Hayward–Hitchings Cluster

*(How to read a deep-dive entry: every statement carries its evidence grade, defined in §0 — [S] primary record, [A] published authority, [PR] peer-reviewed, [B] compiled/tree, [NA] unverified, [user-primary] supplied by the family. ⚠ or 'flagged' marks a live conflict the research has not resolved; 'logged, not adopted' means the claim is recorded but carries no weight. Dates run 28–30 September 2026 unless stated.)*

*(Research pass: the maternal side of the Hawkes proof — the family of Olive Willis, wife of James Hawks — taken from a two-name lead to its immigrant walls. Every statement below is graded. Nothing here removes or supersedes §13; it extends it.)*

### 13A.1 The Willis line — confirmed end to end, and a clan intermarriage discovered

**Silas Willis & Sarah Hayward — confirmed at [A] by Mitchell's *History of Bridgewater* itself (family register No. 28), read in full on 28 September 2026:** *"Silas (s. of Joshua 13.) m. Sarah, D. of Josiah Hayward, and had Sarah 1746, Silas 1748, Adam 1750, Edmund 1753, Olive 1756, Hannah 1758, Betty 1761, Abraham 1764. This family all removed from town."* [A — Mitchell, 1840]

Mitchell's entry names Sarah's parentage directly and gives the complete sibling set — Sarah (1746), Silas Jr. (1748, matching the FamilySearch record), Adam (1750), **Edmund (1753 — the judge's namesake, matching the file's 'Edmund Willis b. June 24, 1753')**, Olive (1756), Hannah (1758), **Betty (1761 — matching Betty m. Moses Hawks, 1787)**, Abraham (1764) — and records the family's removal from town, the start of the Worcester County/Hardwick trail. The Washburn family genealogy (compiled from Bridgewater vital records, deeds, and Josiah Hayward's probated will) (compiled from Bridgewater vital records, deeds, and Josiah Hayward's probated will) states: *"Sarah⁵ Hayward, born on 3 Nov. 1724 in Bridgewater, married Silas⁴ Willis, son of Joshua³ and Experience (Barbour) Willis, of Bridgewater. He was a grandson of Comfort² Willis, of the same town. They supposedly moved out of Bridgewater, probably to Worcester Co., MA, as most of his children were married in New Braintree or Hardwick."* [A — VR-sourced compilation, independent of Mitchell]

Two independent sources thus agree on the marriage and the removal — and the Hardwick/New-Braintree trail explains, in documentary sources for the first time, how the Willis family appears in Hardwick, where James Hawks married Olive Willis in 1776.

**The chain, every link graded:**
```
Dea. JOHN WILLIS (England, by 1637 – will proved 1693, Bridgewater)
   m. Elizabeth Hodgkins, widow of William Palmer Jr. (maiden name Hodgkins) — the pair recorded at Duxbury 1637 [A — Mitchell]; the Jan. 2, 1637/38 marriage date [FS — flagged]
├── COMFORT WILLIS (b. ~1641–48 – d. 1702), m. Hannah — of "Comfort's bridge," Philip's War trooper [A]
│    └── JOSHUA WILLIS (b. ~1682 – d. 1758/9), m. Experience Barbour, Feb. 4, 1706/07, Bridgewater [A]
│         └── SILAS WILLIS, m. Sarah Hayward — removed to Worcester Co.; children married
│              at New Braintree & Hardwick [A]
│              └── OLIVE WILLIS (b. 1756), m. James Hawks, Mar. 7, 1776, Hardwick [S/B]
└── HANNAH WILLIS, m. NATHANIEL HAYWARD [S — Dea. John Willis's will, 1692/93, child list; A — Mitchell's Willis register]
     └── Nathaniel Hayward Jr., m. Elizabeth Crossman
          └── JOSIAH HAYWARD (b. Mar. 1, 1688/9 – d. Feb. 4, 1764), m. Sarah Kinsley, July 19, 1715
               └── SARAH HAYWARD (b. Nov. 3, 1724), m. Silas Willis [A]
```

**The intermarriage (new finding):** Mitchell's Willis entry names Dea. John Willis's children as *"John, Nathaniel, Joseph, Comfort, Benjamin, Hannah, wife of Nath'l Hayward, Elizabeth m. a Harvey, Sarah wife of John Ames"* [A]. Hannah Willis, wife of Nathaniel Hayward, was Dea. John Willis's own daughter. Therefore **Olive Willis's parents were kin**: Dea. John is Silas's great-grandfather (John → Comfort → Joshua → Silas) and Sarah Hayward's great-great-grandfather (John → Hannah → Nathaniel Jr. → Josiah → Sarah) — second cousins once removed. The user descends from **Deacon John Willis twice**, through both his son Comfort and his daughter Hannah. This is the same four-family marriage web the file has documented among the Hawkes–Duntons (Hannibal 1815), the Willis–Hawleys, and now three Willis–Hayward unions across two generations (James & Olive 1776; Moses & Betty 1787; Mercy & Isaac 1807).

**Corroborations:** Silas Willis Jr. (Olive's brother), b. July 28, 1748, Bridgewater, m. Ruth Wright, Nov. 29, 1778, Hardwick — his record names his mother Sarah Hayward [FS]. The Cullen family ahnentafel independently gives the identical chain (Silas s/o Joshua & Experience Barbour, grandson of Comfort) and the three sibling marriages the file identified: Olive (m. James Hawkes 1776), Edmund (b. June 24, 1753, m. Mercy Fuller 1777 — the judge's namesake), and Betty (b. 1761, m. Moses Hawks 1787) [B — tree-grade, now twice corroborated at the generations it covers].

### 13A.2 The Hayward line — pushed to the immigrant on a PRIMARY passenger record

**Thomas Hayward of Aylesford, Kent — tailor.** The surviving minister's certificate for the ship ***Hercules***, dated March 14, 1634/5, enrolls: *"Thos Hayward of Aylesford in Kent Taylor & Susanna his Wief"* with children Thomas, John, Elizabeth, Susan, and Martha [S — Hotten, *Original Lists*; PR — Anderson, *Great Migration* 3:288; NEHGR 75:219]. Cambridge grantee 1635; Duxbury by 1638; freeman **1646 (Mitchell) — 1647 in the earlier note; discrepancy flagged**; **original proprietor of Bridgewater**; will dated June 29, 1678, inventory March 8, 1680/1, will exhibited to the court June 7, 1681 [A — Mapes]; witness Thomas Hayward Jr., not a legatee [A — Mapes]. Mitchell's own register entry: *'Thomas Hayward came from England in the same vessel with John Ames and settled in Duxbury before 1638... he d. 1681: his will dated 1678: no wife living'* [A]. The will mentions lands previously given to **son Nathaniel** — the line's next generation [S]. Parentage unknown; the Aylesford parish registers do not survive before 1654 — a likely permanent English wall [PR]. **New details from the Mapes booklet and Mitchell's town history [A]:** Thomas may have preceded the family — Mitchell records *'he had, perhaps, been here as early as 1632, coming with Winslow, in the William and Francis'* (a 'Thomas Haeward' is on that 1632 list; the identification is flagged, not adopted). His son **Thomas Jr.** — the most honored man of the town: Lieutenant 1667, Captain 1692, Magistrate, one of the Governor's Assistants (1690), Justice; *'his death was occasioned by a fall from his horse, 15 Aug. 1698'* while on court business. The 1907 *Dunham Genealogy*'s claim of a daughter 'Mehitable' m. Daniel Dunham is rejected per TAG (1954): Daniel Dunham's wife was Hannah, maiden name undetermined. Thomas's wife **Susanna's kinship is now documented, her maiden name still unproven** — the will of **Peter Towne** bequeaths to his *'first cousins at Bridgewater'* — **Nathaniel Howard, Joseph Howard, Elisha Howard, and Mary Mitchell**, children of Thomas Hayward and Susanna [PR — GM 7:78; NEHGR 20:370]. The cousinship is proven by the will itself; whether it runs through Susanna as a sister of William Towne, or through Thomas, remains open — 'Towne' remains a flagged hypothesis, now resting on documented cousinship. **The will and inventory, at full detail [S]:** the will (June 29, 1678) names sons Joseph and Elisha, grandson Joseph, and lands previously given to Nathaniel; witnessed by **William Brett Sr. and Thomas Hayward Jr.** — Brett's daughter Alice being Joseph Hayward's first wife. The inventory (March 8, 1680/1; £46 11s 9d, no real estate) lists his library — Sheppard 'upon the parable of the ten virgins,' Dod 'upon the Commandements,' Cooper's 'Jacobs wrestling with God,' Byfield's 'marrow' — a tailor's Puritan bookshelf. Probated June 7, 1681; he died probably February 1680/1. **King Philip's War, in Comfort Willis's own manuscript [A]:** the skirmish narrative names **John Willis — Dea. John's son — and his brother-in-law Nathaniel Hayward** among the Bridgewater men who, with Capt. Church, 'fought with them, and took seventeen of them alive' — the in-laws side by side, more than a decade after the 1664 marriage. **The Sandwich question — CORRECTED (29 September dive), two Sandwiches kept distinct:** the *Hercules* was *'of Sandwich, John Witherly, Master'* — her home port **Sandwich, Kent** (Mapes, citing the Sandwich records). But the 'Sandwich, whence he came' of the 1656 Bridgewater record is **Sandwich, MASSACHUSETTS** (Barnstable County, Plymouth Colony): brother **Nathaniel Willis was constable there in 1640–41** before removing to Duxbury (WikiTree PGM profile Willis-93) — NOT Sandwich, Kent. The earlier 'Kent–Sandwich orbit' framing is corrected accordingly. What stands: the ship's Kent port (Thomas Hayward of Aylesford, Kent, sailed in her), and John Ames of the same vessel, whose descendants intermarried the Willises for generations. Flagged collateral note: Thomas Hayward is a documented presidential ancestor (Taft) through other Bridgewater branches — kin by shared immigrant only, logged, not adopted.

**The descent to Olive's mother — verified verbatim against Mitchell's Hayward register (pp. 181–190, located via a second transcription on this pass):** No. 3: *'Nathaniel (s. of Thomas 1.) m. Hannah, D. of Dea. John Willis, and had Nathaniel 1664, John, Thomas, Samuel, Benjamin, Elisha, Patience'* [A]; the marriage also in Torrey: *'HAYWARD, Nathaniel (ca. 1640–) & Hannah [WILLIS], dau. John; by 1664; Bridgewater'* [A — *New England Marriages to 1700*, 2:731]. No. 7: *'Nathaniel (s. of Nathaniel 3.) lived in E.B. (at Joppa): his wife Elizabeth, had Josiah 1688, Nathaniel 1690, Isaac 1691, Hannah 1694, Sarah 1696, Elizabeth 1698, Timothy 1700, Mercy 1702, Mary 1705, Susanna 1707, Bethiah 1711: his will 1734'* [A]. No. 20: *'Josiah (s. of Nathaniel 7.) m. Sarah, D. of Samuel Kinsley, 1715, and had Josiah 1717, Nathan 1720, Abraham 1722, Sarah 1724, Martha 1727. He then m. Sarah, wid. of Theodosius Moore and D. of John Prior, 1738... he d. 1764. — **Sarah m. Silas Willis a. 1745**'* [A] — Mitchell records the marriage in both registers. Josiah's will (1759, proved 1764) names *'sons Josiah and Nathan Hayward, and daughters Sarah Hayward and Hannah Hayward'* [A/S via the Washburn compilation's probate citation]. **Corrections this verification forces:** (1) Sarah Kinsley's parentage — **FULLY RESOLVED at [A] (29 September)**: her father **Samuel Kinsley (d. intestate, Dec. 17, 1713, Easton; estate divided 1722 per Bristol County Probate 4:141–147)**, her mother **MARY WASHBURN (b. 1661 – d. Feb. 28, 1740, Easton)** — daughter of **John Washburn & ELIZABETH MITCHELL** (m. 1645), per Mitchell's Washburn register and the probated estate [A]. The 1722 division names the children: Samuel, Benjamin, **Hannah (m. Edward Hayward, Esq., 1714)**, **Sarah (m. Josiah Hayward, 19 July 1715 — our line)**, **Mary (m. Thomas Willis, 1716)**, Susanna, Abigail, Bethiah — three Kinsley sisters marrying Hayward, Hayward, and Willis. **The Mitchell line — the pass's major find:** Elizabeth Mitchell was the daughter of **EXPERIENCE MITCHELL — who came on the *ANN* in 1623, AFTER LEYDEN** — giving the user a *second documented Pilgrim-ship line in the direct ancestry*: user → … → Mary Hawkes → James Hawks & Olive WILLIS → Silas Willis & Sarah Hayward → Josiah Hayward & Sarah Kinsley → Samuel Kinsley & Mary Washburn → John Washburn & Elizabeth Mitchell → **Experience Mitchell (*Ann*, 1623; at Leyden with the Pilgrims)** [A — Mitchell registers + Mayflower Society-grade]. (The Washburn immigrant's English origin — once guessed as Wicken, Cambridgeshire — is now RESOLVED to Bengeworth, Worcestershire, per the 29 September dive; the Bridgewater Washburns are a separate line from the Plymouth John Washburn.) **Presidential kin-note [B — published lists]:** **both Presidents Bush descend from Mary (Washburn) Kinsley — the user's own ancestress and her husband Samuel Kinsley** — through a different Kinsley child; the user and both Bushes thus share the ancestors **John Washburn Jr. & Elizabeth Mitchell** (Cooke's granddaughter). (2) Nathaniel Jr.'s wife's maiden name — Mitchell gives only *'his wife Elizabeth'*; the Washburn compilation's *Crossman* identification is **rejected**: two independent VR-sourced compilations give her as **Elizabeth FOBES, b. Apr. 26, 1657, Bridgewater – d. Dec. 26, 1739, aged 82 — daughter of John Fobes (the Bridgewater immigrant) & Constant Mitchell** [B — jskent genealogy, the henryadamsofbraintree Fobes compilation, and the heatonbrown compilation — three independent, all citing the Bridgewater VRs]. **The date conflict — RESOLVED in favor of Apr. 26, 1657 – Dec. 26, 1739 (29 September dive):** two VR-citing compilations now agree — henryadamsofbraintree (citing the Bridgewater VRs and *New England Marriages Prior to 1700*, p. 272) and heatonbrown (independently: 'Nathaniel Hayward and Elizabeth Fobes'); the mdhervey variant (Mar. 6, 1657 – September 25, 1737) is the outlier, presumed a tree error, carried as such pending the direct VR read. **The two-Elizabeth disambiguation — CONFIRMED [B]:** Edward Fobes & Elizabeth Howard's daughter was **Elizabeth Fobes b. Mar. 20, 1677 – d. Feb. 12, 1758, m. Joseph Keith, 1694** (the doigsden Howard/Keith records) — a different woman from our Elizabeth (1657–1739); Savage's ghost is fully exorcised. **Family detail:** her brother **Joshua Fobes fell with Capt. Michael Pierce at Pierce's Fight** near Attleborough, March 1676 (per Cutter; his '1767' a misprint) [B]. Nathaniel d. July 11, 1734, 'his will 1734' per Mitchell ✓. The Crossman confusion likely arose from the documented *Joseph Hayward jur & Sarah Crossman* marriage of September 9, 1703 [A] — a different Hayward branch (Joseph s. of Joseph s. of Thomas 1). Carried as **Elizabeth (Fobes) Hayward**, Fobes at [B] pending the direct Bridgewater VR read. **The Fobes connection braids the web tighter — and is now primary-source confirmed:** Mitchell's Fobes register names John Fobes's children as *'John, Edward, Mary, Caleb, William, Joshua, and **Elizabeth**'* [A]; the brother **Edward Fobes (1651–1732) married Elizabeth Howard** — Mitchell's Howard register: Lt. *'John [Howard] m. Martha, D. of Thos. Hayward,'* with daughter *'Elizabeth m. Edward Fobes'* [A] — and the 1701 Howard estate agreement survives **verbatim**: *'This above written is the mutual agreement of the sons, sons-in-law, and daughters of the above-named Lieut. John Howard, late of Bridgewater, deceased... Signed... by Her **MARTHA X HOWARD**... **Edward Fobes, Elizabeth Fobes**, Zachaeus Packard in behalf of himself and Sarah his wife, Henry Kingman... and Edward Mitchell as guardian of the children of James Howard, deceased... the above-named parties... came personally before me, the subscriber, Judge of Probate for the County of Plymouth... WILLIAM BRADFORD. Recorded, October 24, 1701'* [S]. Edward's wife was thus the granddaughter of the immigrant Thomas Hayward (her mother Martha was his daughter). **The Savage conflict — RESOLVED (GM verification, 28 September):** Savage's *'Joseph [Hayward]... m. Eliz. d. of Edward Fobes'* is a ghost. The Great Migration profile (3:292) gives Joseph Hayward (s. of Thomas 1) **three wives: (1) by 1673 Alice Brett** — daughter of Elder William Brett, the very man before whom John Fobes made his nuncupative will in 1661 — **(2) an unnamed wife whom Mitchell records between Alice and the third, and (3) by 1682/3 Hannah Mitchell** (daughter of Experience Mitchell), by whom he had Mary (1685), Thomas (1687), Edward (1689), Hannah (1691), Susanna (1695), Peter (1699), Abigail (1702). No Elizabeth Fobes anywhere in his record; Savage's children list belongs to another branch. (3) The elder Nathaniel was American-born (b. ~1639, Duxbury [PR — GM]) and married Hannah Willis by 1664 [A — Torrey]; Dea. John's will (dated **June 15, 1692**) calls Hannah *'my eldest daughter Hannah Hayward'* [S — PPR 1:169; Gen. Adv. 3:93]. Whether the Kinsley–Washburn line connects to the famous Maine Washburns (McKinley's line per standard compilations) remains flagged, not adopted.

### 13A.3 The Hitchings line — the pass's best find: a primary will

**Daniel Hitchins of Lynn — will dated March 1, 1734/5, recorded April 7, 1735** — names: *"my daughter Susanna Hawkes, the wife of Moses Hawkes, one hundred pounds currant payable money of New England"* to be paid by son Daniel [S — the will; Essex County probate]. The will also names sons Daniel (eldest), Elkanah, and Timothy, and daughters Elizabeth Haven, Mary Hitchings, Hannah How, and Sarah Giddings; witnesses include Thomas Hawkes [S/B].

This is primary-source confirmation of **Susannah Hitchings (b. March 22, 1710/11, Lynn)** as the wife of Moses Hawkes — the user's direct ancestress on the MF17:60 chart, previously carried at Society-chart grade alone. Her parents, per the Lynn-VR-sourced Hitchings genealogy [B]: **Daniel Hitchings (b. ~1660 – d. Jan. 1734/5, Lynn)**, the will's testator (styled 'junior' in the 1732 deed while his father, Daniel b. ~1632 d. 1731, lived — the Sr./Jr. designations shifted at the father's death), and **Susannah Townsend** (m. 1708 — the published Essex Institute abstract reads: *'Daniel Hitchings of Lynn and Susannah Townsend **of Maiden**. Dec. 18, 1708'* [A/S — EIHC]; the Lynn-VR-sourced genealogy gives Oct. 19, 1708 — the day discrepancy is flagged, the year secure). The will's witnesses were Joseph Haven, William Taylor, and **Thomas Hawkes**; the 1732 Hitchings family deed (Essex Deeds 58:236) was witnessed by **Thomas and Jonathan Hawkes** — the two families bound in paper as in marriage. Flagged kin-note [B]: the testator's step-mother (his father's second wife) was **Sarah Cushman, daughter of Thomas Cushman & Mary Allerton** — an Allerton granddaughter by marriage into the Hitchings household one generation above the user's ancestress; logged, not adopted as descent.

**English line — verified at [A]:** the printed transcript of the Cam parish registers (*Gloucestershire Parish Registers: Marriages*, from the first register book, 1569–1708) records verbatim: *'Gyles HYTCHINGS & Alice TROTMAN — 12 May 1592'* [A]. Alice's parentage stands in the same register: *'Thomas TROTMAN & Anne TYNDALL — 13 Apr. 1570'* — Thomas Trotman of Stinchcombe and Anne Tyndale, of the Tyndale family long seated beside the Berkeleys of Berkeley Castle; the Trotman–Tyndale–Hitchings clans intermarried at Cam for generations (the register shows the name-cluster throughout 1570–1612) [A]. What the marriages volume does not give is Gyles's own baptism (the register's christenings occupy separate pages) — his parentage remains open, with other HYTCHINGS in the same register (1587–1602: John Turner & Jane HYTCHINGS; William HYTCHINGS; Thomas HYTCHINGS; John HYTCHINGS & Jane Sharpe) marking the clan. Their son Daniel (b. ~1597) was father of the brothers Joseph and Daniel who settled at Lynn [B]. **IDENTIFIED — the register is KING'S STANLEY itself (29 September dive) [A — Glos PR vol. 15, archive.org gloucestershirep15phil]:** the block containing Raffe Hitchins also holds the Beccotte entries (the King's Stanley family — 'William May & Margarett Beccotte, Jan. 30, 1578') and the register's own gap-note, placing it squarely in the King's Stanley sequence — the same parish as the Parke cluster. So: ***'Raffe Hitchins & Alice Maneringe, 25 Nov. 1578,' King's Stanley*** — Gyles Hitchings's family IN THE PARKE PARISH. And the same register holds ***'Henry Townsend & Jane Howsmaid, 16 Nov. 1585'*** — a **TOWNSEND in the King's Stanley register** [A]: the user's three Gloucestershire lines — **Hitchings, Townsend, Parke** — all appear in the ONE parish register. (This Henry is a different man from Henry of Bracon Ash, who m. Margaret Forthe in London 1590 — flagged; but a Townsend family in the Parke parish is a structural fact.) Raffe is a flagged father-candidate for Gyles or his brothers (Gyles b. ~1562 slightly predates the marriage, so Raffe is more likely a brother or uncle of Gyles's father — the relationship is open). **Register-gap warning carried:** the vol. 15 transcript itself notes *'[A gap here to 1599, though no page is missing]'* — Glos registers of this era have real gaps, and Daniel's ~1597 baptism may fall inside one; the Ancestry Gloucestershire collection (which cross-fills from Bishops' Transcripts) remains the route. The vol. 15 register also carries later Hitchings/Hitchens marriages ('William Townsend & Sarah Hitchens, 12 Oct. 1818'; 'James Jordan & Elizabeth Hitchings, 28 Oct. 1771') — the clan's Glos web extends across parishes. **The wider Glos Hitchings map (WikiTree index) [B]:** North Nibley (Henry b. ~1620; Samuel 1651; Mary, bpt. Aug. 18, 1666 — **who married a Carpenter** [flagged orbit-note]; Elizabeth ~1648; Johnathon 1657), Bisley (James Giles 1804), Sherston Magna (Giles 1773), Frampton on Severn, Pucklechurch — Hitchings families saturate the Cam–Stinchcombe–North Nibley corridor. **THE HITCHINGS ATLAS, THREE COUNTRIES (29–30 September dive) [B — WikiTree's Hitchings/Hitchins indexes]:** (1) *The Glos map, itemized:* **North Nibley — Henry Hitchings (b. abt. 1620), Elizabeth (abt. 1648), Samuel (bpt. Nov. 2, 1651), Johnathon (Dec. 6, 1657), and MARY (HITCHINGS) CARPENTER (bpt. Aug. 18, 1666)** — the North Nibley cluster includes a Hitchings woman who married a CARPENTER [orbit-note]; Ampney St Peter (William bpt. 1788; Thomas Ritchings 1785; **Sarah (Hitchings) TOWNSEND, bpt. bef. July 18, 1790** — a Hitchings–Townsend marriage in Glos!); Siddington; Ampney St Mary; Bisley; Sherston Magna; Frampton on Severn; Pucklechurch; Montpelier. (2) *The American thread:* **Susana (Hitchins) HAWKES, b. Mar. 22, 1711, LYNN, Massachusetts — a Hitchins woman married into the HAWKES family of the user's own ancestral town** [flagged orbit-note, weighed at zero — but the name-pair in the name-town is logged]; Hannah (Hitchings) Haven (b. abt. 1661, Lynn). (3) *The Welsh line:* **LEWIS HITCHINGS, b. abt. 1585, of Newton & Anceysdown, BOSHERSTON, PEMBROKESHIRE, Wales** — the deepest single Hitchings date the atlas holds, with William (abt. 1610), John (abt. 1635), Edward Francis (abt. 1660) following at Bosherston — a documented Welsh family two generations behind Gyles of Cam. The name's three-country footprint (Glos–Wales–New England) is now mapped, and the Glos–Lynn corridor holds both a Hawkes marriage and the user's own line.

May 5, 1646, Watertown, baptized Boston First Church, May 31, 1646 — daughter of Samuel Davis & Ann Norcross), married ca. 1664** [B — the compiled Townsend register, consistent with the Lynn-VR-sourced Hitchings genealogy]. Susannah was born at Boston Nov. 5, 1672, died at Lynn May 12, 1737 [B]. **The English Townsend line — now documented to 1568 (29 September dive):** Susannah's grandfather was **Thomas Townsend (bapt. Jan. 8, 1594/5, Bracon Ash, Norfolk – d. Dec. 22, 1677, Lynn, MA, aged 83; husbandman; Puritan)** — the IMMIGRANT, son of **Henry Townsend Esq. (bapt. May 31, 1568, Bracon Ash, Norfolk – d. Aug. 22, 1625, Gedding, Suffolk, aged 57) & Margaret FORTHE (m. at St. John Zacharies, London, Nov. 5, 1590, by Rev. Ambrose Golden)** [B — WikiTree Townsend-134; the Sam Behling rootsweb register; FS; the Bracon Ash baptisms, the London marriage, and the Gedding burial are parish-register grade cites pending direct reads]. Henry married (2) Anne Calthorpe. The immigrant's siblings: Robert and Elizabeth [B]. **The Raynham Townshends — the public line behind Sir Robert, graded (29 September dive):** the Townshend family seat, Raynham Hall, Norfolk (raynham.co.uk: *'nearly 900 years at Raynham'* — the estate tradition; the documentary record per Archives Hub runs from **the early sixteenth century**, when the family acquired some twenty manors at the Dissolution): **Sir Roger Townshend (c. 1478–1551, Kt.)**, who commissioned the present Hall early in the 17th century; his younger brother **Sir Horatio Townshend, 1st Viscount Townshend of Raynham (1682)**; **Charles 'Turnip' Townshend, 2nd Viscount (1674–1738)** — the Agricultural Revolution, the Townshend Acts that triggered the American Revolution; **Thomas Townshend, Lord Sydney** — for whom the city of Sydney is named; the **Marquises Townshend** to the present. **The Raynham line, documented deep (29 September dive) [A — DNB/ODNB/History of Parliament]:** the Townshends *'settled in Norfolk as sheep-farmers in the mid-fourteenth century'* (HoP) — **the documented floor stands at c. 1350**; **John Townshend of Raynham (d. 1465), 'a prosperous yeoman farmer'** (ODNB) m. Joan, daughter and heiress of **Sir Robert Lunsford** of Romford; their son **Sir Roger Townshend (c. 1430–1493), justice of Common Pleas, 'founder of the Townshend family'** (DNB) — his will survives (*'My body to be buried in the Chapel of St. Katherine, in the Church of St. Mary of Raynham...'*, 14 Aug. 1493); his son **Sir Roger Townshend of Raynham (d. 25 Nov. 1551)** m. **Amy/Anne de Brewse** (d. 25 July 1551, daughter of William de Brewse of Wenham Hall); and among his children, recorded in the Visitation pedigrees, **Thomas Townshend of TESTERTON (d. before 21 Nov. 1573)** — the Testerton cadet. **THE STRUCTURAL FIND:** the user's tree (Townsend-135) already carried **Testerton, Norfolk** ancestors behind Henry of Bracon Ash — Thomas Townshend of Testerton is now the prime documented candidate for the user's Townsend line [B — claim-to-test, upgraded from guess to named candidate]. Behind John (d. 1465), the Visitations print **Lodowic 'de Towneshend' m. Elizabeth de Haywell → Walter m. Matilda de Scogan → Roger m. Katherine Adtherton → Thomas m. Elianora Payne** [NA — Visitation-grade, unverified]. Later color (all [A-HoP]): Sir John Townshend (d. 1603, killed in a duel); Lady Jane (Stanhope) Berkeley, the remarkable grandmother who bought the wardship for £4,000; the 1st Viscount, 'Turnip' Townshend, Lord Sydney. **The name's floor, documented (29 September dive) [A/B — the Hundred Rolls and Bardsley]:** Townsend/Townshend is a **topographic** name — *'dweller at the town's end'* (Old English *tún* + *ende*) — first recorded as **'Geoffrey de le Tuneshende,' Norfolk, 1273** (Hundred Rolls); *'traced to the year 1377, in gentle degree at Snoring Magna'* (Great Snoring, Norfolk); *'in 1398, John Townshend settled at Raynham, which according to some accounts accrued to them by the heiress of **Havile**'* — the estate's founding purchase-marriage. Leland's famous verdict on the family's rise: the grandfather of the then-head *'was a mean man of substance'* — the Townshends were upward-mobile yeomen, not ancient gentry, and the file says so. **The honest floor: the name records from 1273; the family's gentle floor from 1377–1398; no medieval ancestry behind Lodowic's generation beyond the unverified Visitation pedigree.**

**Convergence flagged (weighed at zero):** a Townshend daughter (Catherine) married John Anguishe of South Raynham **& Foulsham** — the same Foulsham where the user's Poppy manors lay; the two Norfolk threads touch at Foulsham, logged as geography, not kinship. **Name-correction logged:** the tree material's 'Sir Robert Townshend' of the 1555 heirship is carried against the documented **Sir Roger (d. 1551)** — the Bracon Ash heirship's exact referent is flagged [B].

**The Forthe thread — a Winthrop connection by marriage (flagged):** Margaret Forthe, daughter of Robert Forthe, was per the compiled sources a **cousin of Mary Forthe, first wife of Governor John Winthrop** of Massachusetts [B — the Winthrop marriage itself is documented; the cousinship is compilation-carried, queued]. Henry's own parents: **Thomas Townsend of Bracon Ash (d. 1591) & Alice (Poppy)** — the POPPY heiress of the manors of Foulsham: the 1555 inquisition naming Henry's father heir, '22 years of age and more,' *'seized of the Manors of Sutton, Swanson and Folsham in Folsham, which came to him via his mother, Lady Alice (Poppy) Townsend'* [B — tree-carried with an IPM citation; the inquisition is queued]. **The generation behind Henry — now documented with the heirship detail [B — WikiTree Townsend-135, citing the inquisition]:** Henry's father **Thomas Townsend of Bracon Ash (b. ~1535 – d. ~1591)** *'was found to be the heir of **Sir Robert Townshend**... at that time 22 years of age, and was seized of the manor of Suton, Swanton and Folsham, in Folsham, with Foxley manor in Twyford, which came to him by Alice his mother, daughter and heir of **Robert Poppy, Esq.**'* — confirming the Poppy heiress and adding the Twyford manor. The elder line behind Sir Robert Townshend is the **Townshend family of Raynham, Norfolk** — the Marquises Townshend of Raynham Hall — covered by the 1909 compiled history *Townsend–Townshend, 1066–1909* [A — archive.org full text], the route to the medieval line. Flagged orbit-note: *'Thomas, eldest son of Sir Robert, in 25th Henry presented the advowson of the church of Twyford, and in 35th Henry he passed this manor to **Rowland Hayward**'* — the Elizabethan Lord Mayor of London; a manor-changing-hands note, not a kinship claim. Behind Henry's mother Alice Poppy stand the Poppys and the Fothes of Digswell [B]. **The emigration question resolved in structure:** the immigrant Thomas was baptized in Norfolk in 1594/5 and died in Lynn in 1677 — the file's old 'Thomas Townsend of Lynn, 1634' note is fully retired; the younger Thomas (b. ca. 1636, 'England or Lynn') is the one ambiguity left.

John Green (1671); Gershom m. Sarah (1678).

**Royal and public-figure kin through these newly confirmed lines (precise relationships only):** none of the new confirmations adds a *blood* royal line beyond §8.4 — the Willis, Hayward, and Hitchings immigrants are yeoman stock (tailor, proprietor-farmer, and yeoman respectively). Collateral public-figure connections now documented at flagged grade: President **Taft** (shared Bridgewater Hayward immigrant — kin by common ancestor Thomas Hayward, several generations removed) and the presidential **Washburn/McKinley** braid through the Kinsley marriage (flagged, not adopted). The royal gateways remain those of §8.4, reached through the Maverick–Gye marriage.

### 13A.7 Source-verification pass (evening, 28 September 2026)

Per protocol, every load-bearing quote in §13A was re-read against its source the same evening:

**Mitchell's *History of Bridgewater*, Willis register (transcription, dunhamwilcox.net, bridgewater14.htm) — read in full and verified verbatim.** Beyond the entries quoted above, the register documents material no prior pass had found:
- **Dea. John Willis's four brothers — Nathaniel, Lawrence, Jonathan, and Francis** — proven from estate papers: Elkanah settled his grandfather Nathaniel's estate in 1686; Dea. John assisted; Elkanah settled his uncle Lawrence's Boston estate in 1703, and in 1704 sold land he described as the estate of his uncle Francis Willis, deceased [A]. Brother Nathaniel was likewise an original Bridgewater Proprietor — 'said to have been the first schoolmaster here' — d. before John, estate settled 1686, children Elkanah and Bethiah [A].
- **The Sandwich origin:** the 1656 Bridgewater record — *'Jonathan Willis, who is at Duxbury for cure, shall not be maintained by Duxbury, but by **Sandwich, whence he came**'* — places the Willis brothers at **Sandwich, Kent**, before Duxbury/Bridgewater [A]. Sandwich is now the lead English origin for Dea. John Willis, superseding the unverified *Paul* 1635 / 'Chettle, Dorset' note (which remains flagged).
- **Comfort Willis (#5)** was a Trooper in King Philip's War and 'left a manuscript, giving an account of one of the skirmishes of that period, which is now unfortunately lost' [A]. His son **Judge Samuel Willis (#12)** removed to Dartmouth and was Judge of the Court of Common Pleas for Bristol County; another Willis son, **Benjamin, 'went to Hardwick'** — a Willis-in-Hardwick thread independent of Silas's family [A].
- The register's contemporary strays — including *'Thomas Willis at Lynn, a Representative there 1634, and afterwards a Proprietor of Sandwich 1637'* — carry Mitchell's own caveat: *'Whether any of these were of the same family we are not able to ascertain.'* The Lynn Thomas Willis is logged as a possible kin-mark, not adopted [A].
- Comfort's sons per Mitchell: **Samuel, Joshua, Isaac** [A]. The register bristles with further Willis–Hayward intermarriages beyond the direct line: John Willis Esq. m. Patience Hayward (1724); Isaac Willis m. Rebecca, D. of Thomas Hayward (1759); David Whitman m. Susanna, D. of Nathaniel Hayward (1738) [A].

**The Fobes and Mitchell registers — read on the accuracy pass [A. tchell, dunhamwilcox bridgewater4/9]:** *'John Fobes (from Duxbury) was one of the original proprietors of Bridgewater, where he settled, and d. a. 1661; made his nuncupative will before William Brett and Arthur Harris'* — with **the will itself surviving verbatim** [S]: *'Being with goodman ffobes of Bridgewater lying sicke and expecting his change... to his two sonnes John and Edward his two eldest sonnes sixty acrees of land... one muskett and his sword to his son John; a lesser gun to his son Edward; a yeerling steer to his Daughter Mary; and the Rest to bee Disposed of by his wife; and Desireth his brother Experience Mitchell of Duxburow to bee what help hee could to his wife and Children'* — William Britt, Arthur Harris. **Two corrections from the PGM deep-read (29 September):** (1) the immigrant died **before June 6, 1660**, at Bridgewater (the court's 'widow Vobes and her husband, late deceased'); the man who 'died at George Allen's in Sandwich' on July 10, 1661 was his **eldest son John**; (2) the Scottish-aristocrat parentage — 'son of Rev. John Forbes of Alford, Aberdeenshire & Christian Barclay' — is **rejected**: reputable Forbes genealogies have that John as a colonel in Dutch service dying childless, and **Y-DNA of Fobes descendants shows no match with Forbes of Corse** [B-rejected — WikiTree Fobes-257, PGM]. The plausible candidate now: **John Vobes, son of Thomas Vobes, baptized at Ashleworth, Gloucestershire, March 23, 1616/7 — orphaned 1628** [B — flagged speculative by the editor; *Ashleworth lies in the same Gloucestershire cluster as the Hitchings of Cam* — the most exciting open lead in the English lines]. **The generation behind the candidate (29 September dive) [B — WikiTree Vobes-1, citing the Ashleworth parish registers]:** the candidate's father was **Thomas Vobes (b. ~1570 – d. before 1626) & presumptive wife Dorothy — children all baptized at Ashleworth, Gloucestershire**, including the John of March 23, 1616/7. The freepages.rootsweb Ashleworth register transcripts (covering 1625–1646) are the direct-read route; the complete Ashleworth register (from 1538) is the next document. The candidate line thus stands four generations from the user — and its patriarch, Thomas Vobes of Ashleworth, was a Gloucestershire contemporary of Gyles Hitchings's own father. *'His wid. Constant, **sister of Experience Mitchell**, m. John Briggs of Portsmouth R.I. 1662. He had John, Edward, Mary, Caleb, William, Joshua, and Elizabeth.'* Experience Mitchell himself came on the ***Ann*, 1623, after Leyden**, and his children married into the Haywards three times: Sarah Mitchell m. John Hayward; Hannah Mitchell m. Joseph Hayward; Edward Mitchell m. Mary Hayward [A].

**THE BRAID OF THE PASS — John Fobes's indenture to Isaac Allerton [S-form]:** the 1660 Plymouth court record has his widow requesting land *'in respect unto the conditions of an indenture made betwixt **Mr. Isaac Allerton** and her husband, John Vobes, late deceased'* — the indenture itself is documentary (the 1972 book's 'passage payment' gloss is disputed by the PGM editor; the indenture is not). **Isaac Allerton is the user's own ninth-great-grandfather through the Hawkes line** — the user's ancestor Allerton thus held the indenture of the father of another direct ancestress (Elizabeth Fobes → Nathaniel Hayward Jr. → Josiah → Sarah Kinsley → Silas Willis → Olive → James Hawks → Judge Edmund → ... → user). Master and servant, both ancestors of the same person. Also in the Fobes cousin-lines: son **William Fobes married Martha Pabodie, granddaughter of John Alden of the Mayflower** — and her predecessor Elizabeth Southworth's will left her 'my next best bed... provided she do not marry William Fobes; but if she do, then to have (only) five shillings' (she did; five shillings it was) [B — color]. **The Sandwich disambiguation (corrected 29 September):** three different referents kept distinct, per collect-don't-adopt — the immigrant John Fobes died at Bridgewater (before June 6, 1660); his *son* John died at George Allen's in **Sandwich, MA** (July 10, 1661); Lt. John Howard is 'reported' born at Sandwich, **Kent** (~1624, unconfirmed); the Willis brothers' Sandwich is **Sandwich, Massachusetts** (Nathaniel, constable 1640–41); the *Hercules* was of Sandwich, **Kent**. Also RESOLVED: jskent's 'elder Nathaniel d. 1681, aged 41' was a conflation — **Dea. John's wife Elizabeth (Hodgkins) Willis died about 1681** [B — WikiTree Hodgkins-4, consistent with the 1692 will's household], while the GM profile (3:292) gives the elder Nathaniel **d. after May 28, 1705** [PR].

**Dea. John Willis's English origin — the three theories consolidated [B — WikiTree PGM profile Willis-93]:** (1) born at **Chettle, Dorset**, son of Nathaniel Willis (1575 – after 1692), dying in London after 1692; (2) the *'John Willis, age 29'* of the ship ***Paul***, Gravesend, April 3, 1635; (3) certified by the minister of **St. Katharine-by-the-Tower, London** — that register shows *'the christening of Johy Wyllis, 4 Oct. 1601, son of Richard Wyllis'*; a Willis family certainly lived by St. Katharine's, and it may be the immigrants' own. Also from the profile: brother **Nathaniel Willis**, constable at Sandwich, MA, 1640–41; brother **Lawrence Willis** m. (2), 1656, **Mary, daughter of Thomas Makepeace of Boston** (a first freeman of Bridgewater; his Boston estate settled 1703). **The 1917 *Willis Family History* — READ (29 September): the full Warwickshire pedigree, with the royal-ancestry chapter.** From the *Visitation of Warwickshire* (1619) and Waters' NEHGR article, the book prints [A]:
```
1 Richard WILLES of Napton, b. ~1350, m. Jona, dau. & heir of John JEAMES (the Jeames
  inheritance running through the KEVERELL heiress — Robert Keverell m. Clemence,
  dau. of Sir Adam of Napton)
2 Thomas Willes of Napton → 3 Richard → 4 Thomas of Priors Marston
5 Richard of FENNY COMPTON, b. ~1468, d. May 1532, m. Joane dau. of Grant of
  Norbrooke — his WILL survives in print ('I Richard Wyllys of hole mynde...')

  Chambre of Williams-cot, Oxon.)
7 Ambrose of Fenny Compton, d. Nov. 1590 (m. Agnes dau. of William Coles of Great
  Preston, Northumberland — GRANDFATHER OF GOV. GEORGE WYLLYS OF CT) and Richard
  (m. dau. of SIR GEORGE BLOUNT, Knt. — the Blounts of Sodington; Sir John Blount m.

  ALFRED THE GREAT and CHARLEMAGNE)
8 Thomas WILLIS of THISTLEWORTH (Isleworth), Middlesex — 'schoolmaster,' b. Fenny
  Compton 1582, St. John's College, Oxford (matric. 1602 aged 19; B.A. 1606, M.A.
  1609); m. MARY TOMLINS OF GLOUCESTER (another Glos thread); schoolmaster at
  Thistleworth; to LYNN, MASS., 1630; back to Thistleworth ~1646; d. 1660
  Children: Thomas (D.D., Chaplain in Ordinary to Charles II); HENRY (b. ~1618,

```

John Willis's line**; Mitchell's caveat stands ('whether any of these were of the same family we are not able to ascertain'). The medieval Willes material (1350 → Blount → Beauchamp → Alfred/Charlemagne) attaches to the LYNN family and to Gov. George Wyllys of CT — carried at that grade, beside the user's line, not on it. **Connection point worth one test:** the Hopewell papers' **George Willis, 'father-in-law of Emanuel Smith'** (1703), sits in the Morris County orbit the Boonton book covers — flagged as the same family-cluster question, next pass.

**The Hitchings will — verified verbatim** against the full transcript (nutfieldgenealogy, 'Surname Saturday ~ HITCHINGS of Lynn,' 4 Oct. 2014, citing Essex Deeds 58:236) [B-authority]. The §13A.3 quotation is accurate; the witness list (Joseph Haven, William Taylor, Thomas Hawkes) and the 1732 deed witnesses (Thomas and Jonathan Hawkes) are added to the record from this source.

**Flagged tension (kept, per protocol):** Mitchell's *Mitchell*-family register (bridgewater10.htm) carries *'Mary perhaps m. Nathaniel Hayward Jr. 1762'* — a Nathaniel Hayward Jr. marrying in 1762 cannot be the father of Josiah Hayward (b. 1688/9) and is presumed a later namesake; Josiah's mother **Elizabeth Crossman** rests on the Washburn compilation [A] and is carried with this flag. **RESOLVED later the same day:** Mitchell's Hayward register (pp. 181–190) was located and read in a second transcription — the entries are quoted verbatim in §13A.2. The same reading widens the documented intermarriage web [A]: John Hayward (s. of Nathaniel 3 & Hannah Willis) m. **Sarah, D. of Nathaniel Willis** — Dea. John's niece — in 1707; Patience Hayward m. John Willis (1724); Bethiah Hayward m. Thomas Willis (probably 1743); Elizabeth, D. of Dea. John Willis, m. a Harvey (Harvey register No. 1). The Willis–Hayward intermarriages documented in Mitchell's register alone now number at least seven across four generations — the clan structure from which the user's line descends twice.

### 13A.11 The Mitchell–Cooke line: the Ann, the Mayflower, and Leyden (28 September 2026)

**Experience Mitchell** (b. ~1603 per Anderson's estimate; Mitchell read his age as 'ae. a. 80' at 1689 — carried at ~1603–1609) [PR — Robert C. Anderson, *Great Migration Begins*; the WikiTree PGM profile]: **origin UNCERTAIN — parents unproved.** The leading theory (Underhill, following the Dexters; the Plimoth-Plantation/NEHGS collaborative profile *'gives credence to the belief'*) makes him a son of **Thomas Mitchell (b. 1566) of Cambridge, England**, who joined Rev. Francis Johnson's church at Amsterdam and then removed to Leyden — m. (1) Maria, (2) at Amsterdam, May 9, 1606, **Margaret Uochin (Digehin)**; by the two birth-year estimates Experience may be the son of either wife [PR-flagged as belief, not adopted]. At Leyden with the Pilgrims; crossed on the ***ANN*, 1623** — the third ship (140 tons); granted **8 acres in the 1623 land division, in partnership with George Morton** (Robert Cushman's stepson — the Allerton–Cushman business orbit); freeman by 1633; m. (1) at Plymouth, **May 22, 1627, JANE COOKE — daughter of FRANCIS COOKE, MAYFLOWER passenger of 1620 and signer of the Compact, and Hester le Mahieu** (who crossed on the Ann with the Cooke children) — children by Jane: **ELIZABETH** (m. John Washburn, 1645 — the user's line), Thomas, Mary; m. (2) Mary (surname genuinely unknown; 'Molton' is undocumented); children: Sarah, Jacob, Edward, John, Hannah. **The feudal setting, stated precisely (29 September dive) [S — History of Parliament; the IPM corpus]:** the HoP biography of **John Washbourne (d. c. 1430) of Little Washbourne** is online (historyofparliamentonline.org, 1386–1421 volume), confirming the family 'held the manor of *Knights* Washbourne… as tenants of the **Beauchamp earls of Warwick**.' The two families meet at the Earls of Warwick — one by knight-service, one (on the unproven parallel line) by descent. The distinction is recorded, not blurred.

**The pre-1219 Washbournes — the manor reaches Domesday (29 September dive) [B — WikiTree Washbourne-11, quoting Mabel Thacher Rosemary Washburn's *Washburn Family Foundations in Normandy, England and America*; the facts check against the Domesday/Beauchamp record]:** the manor of Little Washbourne was held by **URSE D'ABITOT** — Sheriff of Worcestershire, the notorious under-tenant of Odo of Bayeux — in the **Domesday Survey of 1086**; when Henry I seized the Abitot estates from Urse's son Roger, Little Washbourne passed with them to **Sir Walter Beauchamp of Elmley**, husband of Urse's daughter **Emeline d'Abitot** — and the Washbournes held it thereafter *as under-tenants of the Beauchamps*, bearing the Beauchamp 'fess between six martlets' in their own tinctures. Between Domesday and the documented line, the paper trail now has primary documents (29 September dive): a charter of 1137–9 (Regesta Regum Anglo-Normannorum III, 357) records **GODARD, with his son SAMPSON, tithing to the See of Worcester from Wassebryna** — *'Concedo et confirmo donationem Godardi et Samsonis filii ejus de decima de Wassebryna'* [S-charter]; and c. 1163 the **Liber Rubeus of Worcester** records *'In Wasseburne iii hides geldabiles et Sampson de eo'* — Sampson holding three geldable hides **of William de Beauchamp** [S — the Red Book]. Then the entangled Williams, carried honestly: **William de Washeborne, Coroner of Worcester, b. 1175 – d. 1238** [B]; a William d'Wasseburne at Orleton-in-Estham, 1202 [S]; and **William Wasseburn' m. Lucy (d. bef. June 1255), father of Sir Roger** (b. bef. 1234 – c. 1299). **The scholarly debate, logged:** the family scholars dispute whether Godard and Sampson held **Little Washbourne (Worcestershire, the Hundred of Oswaldslow — argued from the charter's Worcester tithe and the Beauchamp/Poher company they keep)** or **Great Washbourne (Gloucestershire)** — carried as live [S/B], the Oswaldslow argument currently the stronger. **Urse d'Abitot, the honest record (29 September dive) [S — Keats-Rohan, *Domesday People*, via the UMich genealogy page; A — Wikipedia's synthesis]:** *'Urso De Abitot, Domesday tenant-in-chief, sheriff of Worcestershire from c. 1069 until his death in 1108; he apparently inherited the lands and offices in England of his brother Robert the Dispencer after 1086. **A Norman from Abbetot, Seine-Maritime, arr. Le Havre, cant. Saint-Romain, he was a tenant in Normandy of the chamberlains of Tancarville.** His son and heir Roger was banished by Henry I in 1114, leaving the daughters of Urse and their husbands Roger Marmion and Walter I Beauchamp as heirs to his lands.'* — a self-made man *'from an undistinguished family... made his way on military reputation'*; probably b. ~1040. **The bear:** his name IS the animal — and *'it is likely that the Beauchamp family's emblem, a bear, derives from their relationship to Urse'*; through his daughter Emeline he is a **documented ancestor of the Beauchamp earls of Warwick** [A]. **The parentage dispute, logged:** Grazebrook — *'Urso is said by some to be the son of Americus, Lord of Abtot, and by others of Thurstan le Dispenser'*; Nash's Temple-Library MS makes him 'son of Almericus de Abtot' — carried at [B], both unproven. **The pre-1086 Tancred chain remains at Rosemary Washburn's grade [B].** **The most exciting lead of the dive [B — Nash's Worcestershire, via Habingdon (temp. Charles I)]:** the d'Abitot cadet branch held Croome of the Beauchamps from Osbert d'Abitot (1283), and *'John the son of William married **Washburne**'* — a recorded d'Abitot–Washburne MARRIAGE: the blood-tie between the Abitot stock and a Washbourne that Rosemary Washburn's direct-descent argument needs, here as a documented marriage claim rather than an assumed line — flagged, not adopted, and the single best next document in the medieval line.

### 13A.13 The Cushman and Delano full confirmations — the family-memory names, documented (28 September 2026)

**CUSHMAN — CONFIRMED in the user's direct line, at full detail [A — Elizabeth French's English research, NEHGR 68:181; Anderson, GMB 3:1715–8; the Plymouth court records]:** The line enters through **Sarah Cushman, who married the user's ancestor John Hawkes of Lynn (b. 1633)** — and Sarah was the daughter of **Elder Thomas Cushman (1607/8–1691) and Mary Allerton (b. Leiden ~1616 – d. Plymouth, Nov. 28, 1699), youngest daughter of ISAAC ALLERTON — the user's own ninth-great-grandfather.** Allerton is thus doubly in the household: by blood through Rebecca Maverick (John Hawkes's first wife) and by marriage through Sarah Cushman. **Thomas Cushman's will (Oct. 22, 1690) names *'Sarah Hoaks'* — the user's ancestress-by-marriage, in the Ruling Elder's own hand** [S]. The full story, confirmed: **Robert Cushman** (bpt. Rolvenden, Kent, Feb. 9, 1577/8, son of Thomas & Elinor (Hubbard) Couchman; grocer's apprentice to George Masters; excommunicated at Canterbury for Separatism, readmitted and made freeman 1605; m. Sarah Reder, Alphege parish, Canterbury, July 31, 1606) — the Leiden congregation's **Chief Agent in London, 1617–1625**, who with John Carver negotiated the patent; he and his son **Thomas** (bpt. St Andrew's, Canterbury, Feb. 8, 1607/8) came on the ***Fortune*, arriving Nov. 9, 1621** — Robert preached the famous sermon of 9 Dec. 1621, obtained the colonists' signatures on the articles they had refused the year before, and sailed back Dec. 13 — the *Fortune* was taken by French privateers; Cushman was imprisoned in France (the signed articles and *Mourt's Relation* survived) and he died in London in the spring of 1625, aged 48, before he could return. Thomas, left as Governor Bradford's ward at 14, became **Ruling Elder of Plymouth in 1649, succeeding William Brewster (43 years in office)**, and married Mary Allerton — **the last survivor of the Mayflower, dead in 1699**. Their children: Thomas, Mary (m. Francis Hutchinson), **Sarah (m. John Hawkes)**, Isaac, Elkanah, Fear, Eleazer, Lydia (m. William Harlow). The Allerton–Cushman Cup rests at Pilgrim Hall; the 25-foot Cushman monument (1858) stands on Burial Hill.

**DELANO — THE PRIMARY RECORDS, RECORD BY RECORD (29 September deep-dive; the user asked for details, not snippets — every entry below is from the document or Bangs' archival work [S — Leiden Erfgoed archives / Lille département archives; PR — Bangs, NEHGR 143:195–9 and *Mayflower Quarterly* 56:2 (2007) 150–62, 78:2 (2012)]):**
- **Jean de Lannoy's baptism — FOUND:** the St Christophe parish register, **Tourcoing**: *'Jean de Lanoy, enfant de Guilbert, baptisé le 9 mai 1575'* — the son of **Guilbert de Lanoy** (the register runs Nov. 1573–July 1577; Lille microfilm 1 Mi. 333 R 6). The French Jean = the Dutch Jan; Guilbert = Gysbert.
- **The parents' church admission:** Jean de Lannoij and Marie Mahieu joined the Leiden Walloon church **by confession of faith, October 14, 1595**.
- **The betrothal, verbatim from the register (NH Ondertrouw, folio C-142, Jan. 13, 1596):** *'Groom: Jan Lano, place of birth Torckangie [Tourcoing]; Bride: Mary Mahieu; Witnesses (groom): Piere du Bu, **Gysbert Lano, father**; Witnesses (bride): Jenne Mahieu, mother; Anthonetta Morth; Waalse Kerk'* — the groom's FATHER stood witness. (The Walloon marriage book itself is lost; the banns are the record.)
- **The children's baptisms (Vrouwekerk register, inv. 270):** **Jenne de Lanno, March 18, 1601** (witnesses Piere du Bu, Ollie le Pla, Jenne le Mahieu, Jenne de Lanno); **Philippe de Lannoij, November 6, 1603** (witnesses *'François Cock, Philippe Marines, Tonnette de Lannoij, Mergueritte de Lannoij'* — Francis Cooke, husband of his maternal aunt Hester). ⚠ Date flag: November 6 (the register/OpenArchives) against December 7 (some secondary sources) — the register governs.
- **The sister's burial (Pieterskerk register, Dec. 16, 1603):** *'Een kind van Jan de Lano'* — marked **P, for Pest**: little Jenne died of the plague nine days after Philippe's baptism; the unnamed entry is carried as likely hers [NA — Tamura Jones' analysis, record-quoting].
- **The mother's remarriage (banns Feb. 18, 1605):** Marie le Mahieu m. Robert Mannoo, woolcomber of Namur (the record names Jan de Lanno as her former husband; Mannoo's previous wife Symone Packette; Marie accompanied by *'Jenne Mahieu, mother, and Anthonette Mahieu, sister'*; friends Jan de Feurne and Guillame van Dale).
- **The Mahieu parents and siblings (Bangs, NEHGR 143):** the sisters' recorded places — Marie of **Lille** (7 miles south of Tourcoing), Jenne of **Armentières**, Anthonette of **Houpline**, Françoise of **Bondues**, Hester of **Canterbury**; their parents — **Jacques Mahieu and his wife Jeanne** — received into Leiden Walloon communion June 10, 1590, with transfer letters from the London Walloon church dated April 30, 1590; Jacques (probable father) witnessed Françoise's betrothal to Daniel Cricket, woolcomber of Sandwich, April 22, 1611. The regional registers (Armentières from 1590, Houpline from 1608, Bondues from 1610) hold no earlier Mahieu entries — the family predates every surviving register.
- **The Leiden name study — the door, closed honestly (Bangs):** 290 children named Delano baptized in Leiden 1600–1700; 91 men registered betrothals; 19 Mennonite civil registrations; *'all these records have been carefully studied. They will not provide further information about Philip Delano and his immediate family.'*
- **The portrait that fooled Eleanor Roosevelt [A]:** the famous 'Marie de Lannoy' whose portrait draws pilgrim-tourists to the Jean Pesynhof (the almshouse built on John Robinson's Leiden home) is a DIFFERENT woman — Marie de Lannoy who married Jean Pesijn, sister of Arnoult de Lannoy, no relation; Mrs. Roosevelt obtained the photograph for FDR in error, and it hangs in the Hyde Park entrance hall with no ancestral connection.

**Philippe's American life, from the earlier dive [A]:** *Fortune*, July 1621; the Cooke family group at the 1627 cattle division; m. Hester Dewsbury (Dec. 19, 1634); their son Thomas m. Rebecca Alden (the 1667 court fine — *'Thomas Delanoy, and his now wife, for committing carnal copulation before marriage'*); m. (2) Mary (Pontus) Glass (William Pontus's daughter; their son Samuel m. Elizabeth Standish, Alexander's daughter); d. between Aug. 22, 1681 and Mar. 4, 1681/2; believed buried in the Myles Standish Burying Ground.

The grandfather Guilbert is the documented floor. **Philippe de Lannoy (Delano)** — bpt. **Vrouwekerk, Leiden, Dec. 7, 1603, with FRANCIS COOKE standing witness at the font** (Cooke being married to the baby's maternal aunt, Hester le Mahieu) — son of **Jan (Jean) de Lannoy of Tourcoing** (bpt. May 9, 1575, St Christophe, Tourcoing; son of **Guilbert de Lannoy & Jeanne du Bus** of the Verdegroque/Wervicq district) and **Marie Mahieu of Lille** (daughter of Jeanne Mahieu; father probably Jacques; the Mahieus of Armentières, Houpelyne, Lille, Bondues; Jan & Marie betrothed Vrouwekerk Jan. 13, 1596 — eight recorded places of residence, *more than for any Mayflower passenger*). Jan died of plague within six months of Philippe's baptism; Marie remarried Robert Mannoo, a woolcomber of Namur (Mar. 6, 1605), and vanishes from the record after 1611. **Philippe came on the *Fortune*, July 1621, joining his uncle Francis Cooke and cousin John Cooke** — and the **1627 division of cattle placed him in the Francis Cooke family group**, the colony's own record of the kinship. Freeman Jan. 1, 1632/3; Pequot War volunteer, 1637; surveyor; twenty juries. m. (1) **Hester Dewsbury** (Dec. 19, 1634); their son **Thomas married Rebecca Alden, daughter of John Alden of the Mayflower** — the 1667 court fined *'Thomas Delanoy, and his now wife, for committing carnal copulation before marriage'* — the most famous court entry in the colony's files; m. (2) **Mary (Pontus) Glass**, daughter of the Walloon refugee William Pontus, and their son **Samuel married Elizabeth Standish, daughter of Alexander Standish** — Myles Standish's line. Philippe d. between Aug. 22, 1681 and Mar. 4, 1681/2; believed buried in the Myles Standish Burying Ground. **'DEARMAND' — INVESTIGATED, VERDICT DELIVERED (29 September dive):** the name as heard is carried in three indexed forms, none of which connects to the user's documented web: (1) **DeArmond/Dearmond = Irish Gaelic Ó Duibhdhiorma**, 'descendant of Duibhdhiorma' (Ancestry [A]); the American DeArmonds are Scots-Irish Presbyterians of Pennsylvania and adjacent colonies [FTDNA]; the 1954 *DeArmond Families of America* (Roscoe Carlisle 'd'Armand') and its Huguenot-nobility story (the d'Armand de Chateauvieux of the French Alps) is demolished by the critical review [B]: 'his entire noble French Huguenot origin story is merely his wishful supposition' — 'names spelled as DeArmond appear to be merely affectations of French origin which was once fashionable.' **THE HOUSE OF LANNOY — the stories are true; the link is not (29 September dive, full verdict):** the noble house is real and splendid, documented from **Gillion de l'Annoit, 13th century** (Wikipedia [A]; the name from *l'Annoy*, 'alderwood' in Picard French): the brothers of Santes/Molembais. uillebert II the traveler-diplomat (1386–1462); Hugo (1384–1456); Baudouin 'le Bègue' (1388–1474) — Governor of Lille for fifty years, XX Founder Knight of the Golden Fleece (1429), painted by Jan van Eyck** — all three courtiers of the Burgundian dukes; **Jean I, killed at Agincourt; Jean II (1410–1493), Stadtholder of Holland and Zeeland, restorer of Louis XI** (whose d. 1493 divided the lands and passed Lannoy out of the family); **Charles de Lannoy, 1st Prince of Sulmona (1487–1527)**, Charles V's First Equerry and viceroy of Naples (the Emperor attended the 2nd Prince's Naples wedding to Isabella Colonna in 1534); **fifteen Lannoys received the Golden Fleece**; the princely branch sold out by 1610 (Sulmona to the Borghese). The claimed American link runs through **Baudouin III de Lannoy, Lord of Tourcoing — 'progenitor of the Tourcoing branch'** — to Philippe's family of Tourcoing. **THE VERDICT: the link 'has never been proven'** (Wikipedia, in so many words), and the surname analysis is devastating [NA — researchthroughpeople, citing documents]: *de Lannoy* is the **9th most common surname in the département du Nord** ('the same as Taylor or Jones' — eighty districts of Tourcoing held de Lannoys in twenty, including servants of the noble house); **the six royal lines ran through the de FRANCHIMONT family, not the Lannoys — who have no known royal connection; and the crucial link (Jean de Franchimont m. Mahienne de Lannoy) was DISPROVED IN 1906, in four documents** — 'over a hundred years ago.' The early claimed pedigree (Hugues de Lannoy, knight of Tournai, **1096**; Gilbert 1171; Hugues 1186) is the 1899 book's grade [B]. **Conclusion carried in the file: Philippe's family is a Tourcoing place-name family of Walloon weavers; the noble house is the family's cousin-orbit of story — real, splendid, and not theirs by any document.**

The claims are logged at the 1899 book's grade [B], weighed at zero.

**JOHN HAWKES OF LYNN (1633–1694) — the full documented life, every link between him and the user now closed [A — Mayflower Families 17:9, 15; WikiTree Hawkes-406; the Essex court and probate records; Smith, *Adam Hawkes of Saugus* (1980)]:** b. ~1633 (the oft-cited 'Charlestown, Aug. 13, 1633' is **rejected by MF17: 'No evidence has been found to support this statement'**); twin of **Susanna Hawkes**, who m. **William Cogswell** — the twin-sister marriage that made the user's Moses Hawke & Margaret Cogswell first cousins. m. (1) **Rebecca Maverick, Lynn, June 3, 1658**; she died in childbirth late 1659 leaving the infant **Moses Hawke (b. 1659) — the user's direct line** — whom Moses Maverick's informal will names: *'Moses Hawks, the child of my deceased daughter Rebecca'* [S]. m. (2) **Sarah Cushman, Lynn, April 11, 1661** — *first cousin of his deceased wife* (Remember and Mary Allerton, sisters) — eight children: Susanna, Adam, Ann, Rebecca, Thomas, Mercy, Ebenezer, John; **November 1675: three older daughters died of scarlet fever in one blow**. **King Philip's War:** private in Major Appleton's command, the Great Narragansett Swamp Fight, Dec. 19, 1675 (Bodge, *Soldiers in King Philip's War*, 406); signatory of the 1685 land petition. **The Dam Case — the most famous lawsuit of old Lynn:** when the new Iron Works owner Samuel Appleton flooded the Hawkes farm, John was *'the only plotter, contriver and secret manager of the cutting or breaking of the great dam at Hamersmith'* — he sent sons Adam and Moses with Joseph Trumbull by night (May 1682); the 1683 trial ended **verdict for the defendant**; and the record preserves the courtroom theater of the 2,500-li. bond that Appleton snatched from Thomas Marshall's hand rather than swear to (Appleton v. Hawkes, Essex County records, fully transcribed in *Hawkes Talks* and Smith 1980, pp. 36–7). Town standing: the 1692 Lynn vote had *'Mr. John Hawkes Senior'* sitting at the table with the town's leadership. **Death and probate:** d. Lynn, **August 5, 1694**; estate Essex Probate #12920 — September 4, 1694 settlement between widow Sarah and sons John, Thomas, Ebenezer; **Moses received the farm his grandfather Adam willed him**; Apr. 8, 1695 Sarah surrendered the administration; John quitclaimed 80 acres to his nephew John (son of his brother Adam, d.v.p.). Orbit-note [NA]: the 1878 *Citizen and Banner* story shows John Hawkes of Lynn and John Browne of Reading inquiring after their **Hadley cousins** — John and Eleazer Hawkes — a separate Hawkes branch, logged as cousins, not merged.

**ADAM HAWKES'S PARENTAGE — the candidate now named [B — flagged, against the PGM's official 'unknown']:** the bolz-family reconstruction (with London and Hingham parish-register citations) makes Adam the son of **John Hawkes & MARY COWPER (m. Jan. 27, 1612, Hingham, Norfolk)** — she bapt. Dec. 23, 1586, St Giles, Cripplegate, London; buried July 3, 1672, Holy Trinity Minories — with brothers **John Hawkes (b. ~1590 – d. June 30, 1662, HADLEY, a founder of Hadley, MA — the Hadley cousins' line)** and **MATTHEW HAWKES (b. ~1610; m. Margaret Nelson; emigrated on the *DILIGENT* of Ipswich, Aug. 10, 1638, with a servant, John Fearing; d. Hingham Dec. 11, 1684)**. **This RESOLVES the file's oldest open flag** (§7.12 and the §12 ledger, from the first runs: '*the **Diligent** (1638), which carried the Pecks and **possibly a Hawke brother***'): the Diligent manifest carries **Matthew Hawkes** — the 'possibly' is now 'documented' [A — Hotten's lists for the manifest; B — for the brotherhood]. The PGM's formal position (parents unproved) is kept as the governing grade until the Hingham registers are read directly.

### 13A.14 The *Fortune* (1621) — the full complement, and the Palmer thread (28 September 2026)

**The ship, at full detail [A — Wikipedia's synthesis of Bradford, Banks, Stratton; Pilgrim Hall; Soule 2023]:** the 55-ton *Fortune* (one-third the *Mayflower*'s tonnage), Master Thomas Barton, sailed July 1621, weather-bound until late August, arrived Cape Cod **November 9, 1621** with ~35 passengers and no provisions — Bradford: *'there was not so much as a bisquit cake… nor pot, or pan to dress any meat in'*. Stayed two weeks; sailed December 13 laden with clapboard and beaver (~£500), captured by a French warship (January 19, 1622 — all hands imprisoned ~a month; back in the Thames February 17, 1622). No passenger list survives; the complement is reconstructed from the 1623 Division of Land (28 names), Banks' *Planters of the Commonwealth*, and Stratton. **The full list** (with the user's web marked): John Adams; William & Elizabeth Bassett; William Beale; Jonathan Brewster (Elder's son); Clement Briggs; Edward Bumpas; John Cannon; William Conner; **ROBERT CUSHMAN & son THOMAS** (the user's line, §13A.13); Stephen Deane; **PHILIPPE DE LANNOY/DELANO** (§13A.13); Thomas Flavel & son; Mr. & Mrs. Martha Ford & children; Robert Hicks; William Hilton; Benedict Morgan; **Thomas Morton** (brother of George Morton — the user's Allerton orbit); Austin Nicholas; **WILLIAM PALMER & son WILLIAM JR.** (THE NEW THREAD, below); William Pitt; **Thomas Prence** (future governor); Moses Simonson (de Lannoy's land partner); Hugh Stacie; James Steward; William Tench; **John Winslow** (m. Mary Chilton, Mayflower); **William Wright** (m. **PRISCILLA CARPENTER — sister of Alice Carpenter, Governor Bradford's wife: the Wrington Carpenter women**, the user's Carpenter-line kin by marriage [A]).

**THE PALMER THREAD [B — prime candidate, structure perfect]:** the Fortune carried *'William Palmer… one of the older passengers, b. ~1581, a nailer… his wife Frances and son William Jr. came over in 1623'* — and **Mitchell's Bridgewater record has Dea. John Willis's wife ELIZABETH as 'widow of WILLIAM PALMER JR., her maiden name Hodgkins.'** If the Duxbury William Palmer (d. 1637) is the Fortune man's son, then the user's step-family — the family of Dea. John's wife — arrived on the *Fortune* and *Anne*, placing a fourth thread of the user's web aboard the Fortune and tying the Willis marriage into the Plymouth kin-cluster. **Flags:** the elder Palmer's origin is given as Stepney, Middlesex (Banks) and Parham, Somerset (other lists) — logged; the identification of 'William Palmer Jr. of Duxbury' with the Anne passenger rests on name, date, and place — prime candidate, not yet proven; the test is the Duxbury/Plymouth vital records. The Fortune–Anne–Mayflower intermarriages aboard this one list (Winslow–Chilton, Prence–Brewster, Ford–Browne, Wright–Carpenter) show the colony's kin-web forming in a single season — the same web the user's whole file documents.
